\hypertarget{arithmetic-reflection-and-polynomial-moments}{%
\subsubsection{1 Arithmetic, reflection and polynomial moments}\label{arithmetic-reflection-and-polynomial-moments}}

Lattice Poisson summation transforms a sextic row into a finite Gauss coefficient; cubic-theta automorphy reflects its completed cubic coefficient. After fixing phases and zeros, we prove completed energy, inverse and mixed plain moments, and the probe's exact Poisson factorization, without assuming a zero-free boundary.

These specialized arguments reconstruct Hailey Collet's \emph{A zero-free half-plane from a single compensating prime}, revision \texttt{0b97bfda\allowbreak{}e9f026d9\allowbreak{}446d0779\allowbreak{}52b2b498\allowbreak{}0700e742}, arithmetic conventions and Appendices A--D (with the source's attribution to OpenAI's GPT-6 Pro). The probe and moment framework originates in OpenAI's manuscripts of 30 September and 5 October 2026; their terminal zero-free claims are not inputs.

\hypertarget{cf:classical-inputs}{}

\label{cf:classical-inputs}

\hypertarget{classical-inputs-and-their-scope}{%
\paragraph{1.1 Classical inputs and their scope}\label{classical-inputs-and-their-scope}}

Classical sieve inputs are Goldmakher--Louvel, \emph{A quadratic large sieve inequality over number fields}, Math. Proc. Cambridge Philos. Soc. 154 (2013), 193--212, arXiv:1112.1642v2, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2, specialized and verified in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.11}{(27)}, and Heath-Brown, \emph{Kummer's conjecture for cubic Gauss sums}, Israel J. Math. 120 (2000), 97--124, Theorem 2, DOI:10.1007/s11856-000-1273-y, specialized in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.19}{(36)}. Both require squarefree \textbf{ideals}, not squarefree rational norms. The quadratic sieve requires a fixed ray modulus and row-independent coefficients.

The cubic theta function, Kubota multiplier and cusp expansions are the unconditional formulas in Dunn--Radziwi\l{}\l{}, \emph{Bias in cubic Gauss sums: Patterson's conjecture}, Ann. of Math. 200 (2024), 967--1057, arXiv:2109.07463v3, §5.1, (5.1)--(5.17) and cusp tables. Their needed coefficients, three cusp sequences and archimedean factors appear in the reflection proof. We use neither its conditional prime asymptotics nor conditional improved sieve.

For a primitive finite-order Hecke character \(\chi\) of \(K=\mathbf Q(\sqrt{-3})\), of conductor norm \(Q\), Hecke's theorem continues \(L_K(s,\chi)\) (entire unless principal) and gives

\hypertarget{cf:hecke-classical}{}

\label{cf:hecke-classical}

\[
\Lambda(s,\chi)=C_\chi^{s/2}\Gamma(s)L_K(s,\chi),\qquad
\Lambda(s,\chi)=\epsilon_\chi\Lambda(1-s,\overline\chi),\qquad
C_\chi=\frac{3Q}{(2\pi)^2},\quad |\epsilon_\chi|=1.
\qquad\text{(1)}
\]

For principal \(\chi\), \(\Lambda\) has only simple poles at \(0,1\). Hecke's theorem also gives finite order, polynomial growth on closed vertical strips after removing poles, and absolute Euler convergence for \(\Re s>1\). The normalization is Goldmakher--Louvel, §3, (3.1)--(3.2), with \((r_1,r_2,|d_K|)=(0,1,3)\) and conductor norm absorbed into \(C_\chi\). Other classical inputs are cubic reciprocity and supplementary laws, finite-field Gauss--Jacobi identities, Fourier inversion, self-dual Poisson summation, Mellin inversion, Stirling and Phragmén--Lindelöf. Their uses, including moving masks, are explicit below and assume no sought zero-free boundary.

\hypertarget{cf:prime-supply-and-boundary}{}

\label{cf:prime-supply-and-boundary}

\textbf{Additional classical inputs used below.} The fixed-ray prime ideal theorem is used with the field and ray modulus fixed before the asymptotic scale. For every integral ray modulus \(\mathfrak m\) of \(K\), every class \(C\) of its finite ray-class group, and every fixed finite excluded prime set \(S\), \[
\#\{\mathfrak p\notin S:(\mathfrak p,\mathfrak m)=1,
 [\mathfrak p]=C,\ N\mathfrak p\le X\}
\sim \operatorname{Li}(X)/h_{\mathfrak m}.
\] A fixed union \(B\) of classes has density \(|B|/h_{\mathfrak m}\). No uniformity in a moving modulus is asserted. For fixed nonnegative, nonzero \(w\in C_c^\infty((1,2))\), partial summation consequently gives \[
\sum_{\mathfrak p\in B} w(N\mathfrak p/P)(N\mathfrak p)^{-5/6}
\sim \frac{|B|}{h_{\mathfrak m}}\frac{P^{1/6}}{\log P}
 \int_0^\infty w(t)t^{-5/6}\,dt.
\] Indeed Stieltjes partial summation against the stated counting asymptotic, followed by \(x=Pt\), gives this formula uniformly on the fixed compact support of \(w\). It supplies the fixed-ray amplifier pool in §7 and the nonzero principal normalizer in §14.3; the latter's all-good-prime pool has density one. Finite excluded primes do not change the asymptotic.

We also use classical Dirichlet nonvanishing on the line \(\Re s=1\): for every modulus \(q\ge1\) and Dirichlet character \(\chi\bmod q\), \(L(s,\chi)\ne0\) when \(\Re s=1\), excluding the principal pole at \(s=1\). Together with absolute Euler convergence for \(\Re s>1\), this is the boundary input in §14.13. An imprimitive character's deleted factors \(1-\chi^*(p)p^{-s}\) are nonzero on \(\Re s\ge1\) because \(|\chi^*(p)p^{-s}|\le p^{-1}<1\). Boundary nonvanishing is a separate classical theorem; it is not deduced here from the functional equation.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:1.1}{}

\label{cf:sec:1.1}

\hypertarget{arithmetic-conventions}{%
\paragraph{1.2 Arithmetic conventions}\label{arithmetic-conventions}}

Put \(\mathcal O=\mathbf Z[\omega]\), \(\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}\), \(\lambda=1+2\omega\), \(q_a=Na\). This norm-Euclidean ring has six units; every ideal prime to \(3\) has a unique primary generator \(\equiv1\pmod{3\mathcal O}\). Ideal sums count it once; element sums retain units. Ideal and lattice counts through norm \(X\ge1\) are \(O(X)\).

For each target character fix, before varying scales or rows, a finite \(S\) containing conductor primes, primes above \(6\), and a small-prime cutoff. Columns avoid \(S\) unless specified; primes outside \(S\) are good. Superscript \(S\) deletes these Euler factors, a star means squarefree ideals, and \(\sum^{(6)}\) means element rows with valuations \(0,\ldots,5\).

At a good prime \(\mathfrak p\), \(\chi_{\mathfrak p}(v)\) is the sextic residue symbol, the sixth root of unity \(\equiv v^{(q_{\mathfrak p}-1)/6}\) at units, extended multiplicatively in the denominator. \textbf{All integer character powers vanish at nonunits}, including zero and negative powers; only an absent modulus-one factor is identically one. Write

\hypertarget{cf:eq:1.1}{}

\label{cf:eq:1.1}

\[
\psi_u(n)=\chi_n(u),\qquad \alpha(a)=a/|a|,\qquad
 e(z)=\exp\!\left(2\pi i\operatorname{Tr}(z/\lambda)\right),\qquad
 d\mu(z)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\,dx\,dy.
\qquad\text{(2)}
\]

Here \(\operatorname{Tr}z=z+\bar z\); \(e(zy)\) is self-dual on \(\mathcal O\), with Fourier kernel \(e(-zy)\). For a zero-extended character \(\chi\), put

\hypertarget{cf:eq:1.2}{}

\label{cf:eq:1.2}

\[
g_\chi(c,h)=\sum_{d\bmod c}\chi(d)e(hd/c),\qquad
 \gamma_j(c)=q_c^{-1/2}g_{\chi_c^j}(c,1)\quad(c\text{ squarefree}).
\qquad\text{(3)}
\]

The inverse-character sum \(\gamma_{-1}\) does not mean conjugation. For odd \(a+b\omega\), define

\hypertarget{cf:eq:1.3}{}

\label{cf:eq:1.3}

\[
\Gamma_{\rm q}(a+b\omega)=\tfrac12(1+i^{-b}+i^a+i^{b-a}),\quad
 G(c)=\overline{\chi_c(4)}\Gamma_{\rm q}(c),\quad
 R(v,w)=\frac{\Gamma_{\rm q}(vw)}{\Gamma_{\rm q}(v)\Gamma_{\rm q}(w)}.
\qquad\text{(4)}
\]

Lemma \protect\hyperlink{cf:finite-phases}{2.1} proves that \(G\) is a finite-ray unit phase, \(R\) a symmetric sign-valued bicharacter, and \(\chi_b(a)=R(a,b)\chi_a(b)\) for coprime primary indices. Expand finite-ray functions into genuine characters before using multiplicativity.

The ideal character \(\psi_u\) is the arithmetic-Frobenius Kummer character of \(K(u^{1/6})/K\), retaining even redundant zeros at primes of \(u\). At a good prime with valuation \(j\), tame inertia has exponent \(\pm j\bmod6\) and conductor exponent one unless \(6\mid j\). At \(S\), Hensel makes deep principal units sixth powers. Thus fixed finite-ray twists of \(\psi_u^{\pm1}\) have conductor norm \(O_S(q_u)\); every nonunit sixth-power-free row prime to \(S\) is nonprincipal.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:1.2}{}

\label{cf:sec:1.2}

\hypertarget{smooth-weights-and-uniformity}{%
\paragraph{1.3 Smooth weights and uniformity}\label{smooth-weights-and-uniformity}}

An annular weight is smooth and compactly supported in \((0,\infty)\), with \(\widehat W(s)=\int_0^\infty W(y)y^s\,dy/y\). Constants may depend on fixed characters, supports and positive losses, never moving arithmetic labels. Real exponents lie in fixed bounded sets. All estimates use finitely many smooth seminorms; multiplying a test by \(y^{b+it}\), for bounded \(b\), costs \((1+|t|)^{O(1)}\).

Profiles are common; arithmetic coefficients are row-independent. Integration by parts gives a compact joint logarithmic profile a Fourier expansion with finite weighted \(L^1\) norm. Minkowski and Cauchy reduce its row norm to separated unit-modulus norm powers. Thus if \(C_j(W)=\sum_nc_{j,n}W(x_{j,n})\) satisfies \(\sum_j|C_j(W)|^2\le M\|W\|_{C^m(I')}^2\) for every common test in \(I'\), then kernels supported in fixed \(I^2\Subset(I')^2\) satisfy

\hypertarget{cf:eq:1.4}{}

\label{cf:eq:1.4}

\[
\left|\sum_jb_j\sum_{n_1,n_2}c_{j,n_1}\overline{c_{j,n_2}}
 K_j(x_{j,n_1},x_{j,n_2})\right|
 \ll M\sup_j\|K_j\|_{C^{2m+4}},\qquad |b_j|\le1.
\qquad\text{(5)}
\]

Fourier decay \((1+|t_1|)^{-m-2}(1+|t_2|)^{-m-2}\) and the common-test bounds leave integrable powers \(-2\). Sobolev on unit intervals in log scale, real power and height similarly permits rowwise smooth parameters after summing common-profile bounds. Choose all internal smooth orders before the external height cutoff.

\hypertarget{cf:finite-arithmetic}{}

\label{cf:finite-arithmetic}

\hypertarget{finite-arithmetic-for-both-transforms}{%
\subsubsection{2 Finite arithmetic for both transforms}\label{finite-arithmetic-for-both-transforms}}

\hypertarget{cf:sec:A.1}{}

\label{cf:sec:A.1}

\hypertarget{gauss-phases}{%
\paragraph{2.1 Gauss phases}\label{gauss-phases}}

\textbf{Lemma 2.1} (Finite Gauss phases). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:finite-phases}{}\label{cf:finite-phases} For squarefree \(n\), and coprime squarefree \(a,b\), the conventions of Section~\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:1.1}{1.2} give}

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.1}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.1}

\[
\begin{gathered}
\chi_b(a)=R(a,b)\chi_a(b),\qquad R(a,b)=R(b,a)\in\{\pm1\},\quad (a,b)=1,\\
\gamma_2(n)^3=\mu(n)\alpha(n),\qquad
\gamma_1(n)\gamma_2(n)=\mu(n)\alpha(n)G(n),\\
\mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n),\\
a_\nu(ab)=a_\nu(a)a_\nu(b)\chi_b(a)^4,
\qquad a_\nu(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\nu(n),\quad (a,b)=1.
\end{gathered}
\qquad\text{(6)}
\]

\emph{Proof.} At a prime \(p\), put \(J(A,B)=\sum_xA(x)B(1-x)\) with zero extensions. Substitution \(u=2x-1\) gives \(\chi_p(4)J(\chi_p,\chi_p)=J(\chi_p,\chi_p^3)\); Gauss--Jacobi and \(\gamma_2(p)\gamma_4(p)=1\) give

\[
\gamma_1(p)\gamma_2(p)
=\overline{\chi_p(4)}\gamma_3(p)\gamma_2(p)^3.
\]

The Jacobi sum \(J(\chi_p^2,\chi_p^2)\) has norm \(q_p\) and is divisible by \(p\), by expanding \(x^{(q_p-1)/3}(1-x)^{(q_p-1)/3}\) modulo \(p\) and summing powers. Its unit follows modulo three: \(\prod_{x\ne0,1}x(1-x)=1\) implies \(\sum j_x\equiv0\pmod3\) for character values \(\omega^{j_x}\), so the sum is \(-1\pmod3\). Since \(p\equiv1\pmod3\), it equals \(-p\), giving \(\gamma_2(p)^3=-\alpha(p)\).

For odd \(c\), Gaussian-regularized Poisson summation gives

\[
|c|^{-1}\sum_{x\bmod c}e(x^2/c)
=\tfrac12\sum_{y\bmod2\mathcal O}e(-cy^2/4)=\Gamma_{\rm q}(c).
\]

For regulator \(e^{-\pi\eta|z|^2}\), the transformed factor has \(r_\eta=1+3q_c\eta^2/16\). Replacing \(r_\eta\) by one costs \(O_c(1)\); divide by Gaussian mass \(\asymp\eta^{-1}\) and let \(\eta\downarrow0\). The square classes \(1,-1,\lambda,-\lambda\) have values \(1,1,i,-i\), whose quotient is the symmetric bicharacter

\[
R((-1)^e\lambda^f,(-1)^g\lambda^h)=(-1)^{eh+fg+fh}.
\]

Grouping by \(y=x^2\) gives \(\Gamma_{\rm q}(p)=\gamma_3(p)\); CRT and cubic reciprocity give sextic reciprocity with \(R\). Also \(\chi_c(4)=(-2/c)_3=(c/(-2))_3\) is a fixed character modulo two. Hence \(G(c)=\overline{\chi_c(4)}\Gamma_{\rm q}(c)\) is a fixed ray phase, and

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.2}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.2}

\[
\begin{gathered}
G(ab)=G(a)G(b)R(a,b),\qquad R(a,a)=\chi_a(-1),\\
G(ba^{-1})=\chi_a(-1)\overline{G(a)}G(b)R(a,b).
\end{gathered}
\qquad\text{(7)}
\]

CRT extends the prime identities to squarefree indices; \(\gamma_2(ab)=\gamma_2(a)\gamma_2(b)\chi_a(b)^2\chi_b(a)^2\) gives the \(a_\nu\) identity. Finally \(\gamma_1(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)\) gives inverse conversion in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.1}{(6)}. All equalities retain nonunit zeros. \(\square\)

\hypertarget{cf:sec:A.4}{}

\label{cf:sec:A.4}

\hypertarget{finite-fourier-correlations}{%
\paragraph{2.2 Finite Fourier correlations}\label{finite-fourier-correlations}}

For primary moduli distinguish the normalized Gauss sum from \(G\):

\[
\mathfrak g(a,h)=q_a^{-1/2}\sum_{x\bmod a}\chi_a(x)e(hx/a),\qquad \mathfrak g(1,h)=1.
\]

For a good prime \(p\), \(P=q_p\), \(j\ge1\), lifting \(x=x_0+py\) gives

\[
|\mathfrak g(p^j,h)|=P^{(j-1)/2}\mathbf1_{v_p(h)=j-1}\quad(6\nmid j),
\]

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.12}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.12}

\[
\mathfrak g(p^j,h)=P^{j/2}\mathbf1_{p^j\mid h}
 -P^{j/2-1}\mathbf1_{p^{j-1}\mid h}\quad(6\mid j).
\qquad\text{(8)}
\]

Here \(v_p(0)=\infty\); a sixth-power character retains its coprimality mask.

Define the full correlation

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.13}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.13}

\[
F(u,v;j)=\sum_{\substack{x\bmod u,\ y\bmod v\\vx-uy\equiv j\pmod{uv}}}
 \chi_u(x)\overline{\chi_v(y)}.
\qquad\text{(9)}
\]

Additive orthogonality gives, with negative Fourier sign,

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.14}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.14}

\[
F(u,v;j)=\frac1{\sqrt{q_uq_v}}
 \sum_{h\bmod uv}\mathfrak g(u,h)\overline{\mathfrak g(v,h)}e(-jh/(uv)).
\qquad\text{(10)}
\]

At \(j=0\), both nonzero residues are units, forcing \(u\mid v\) and \(v\mid u\). Hence

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.15}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.15}

\[
F(u,v;0)=\mathbf1_{u=v}\phi(u).
\qquad\text{(11)}
\]

For complete common-support extraction, let

\[
u=Da,\quad v=Eb,\quad (a,b)=1,\quad (ab,DE)=1,
\]

where every prime of \(D\) also divides \(E\), and conversely. Then

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.16}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.16}

\[
F(Da,Eb;j)=F(D,E;j)R(a,E)R(b,D)R(a,b)
 \chi_a(j)\overline{\chi_b(-j)}.
\qquad\text{(12)}
\]

At primes of \(a,b\), reduction gives \(\chi_a(j)\overline{\chi_a(Eb)}\) and \(\overline{\chi_b(-j)}\chi_b(Da)\). At common primes substitute \(x'=bx\), \(y'=ay\), leaving \(\overline{\chi_D(b)}\chi_E(a)\). The three unit quotients give the three \(R\) factors. All inversions are at units; nonunit \(j\) gives the same zero on both sides.

From \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.13}{(9)}, at common multiplicities \(i,j\) the correlation vanishes unless \(p^{\min(i,j)}\mid h\). For divided frequency \(k\) and \(i=j\), its local value is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.17}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.17}

\[
P^{i-1}\begin{cases}
P-1,&p\mid k,\\
-1,&p\nmid k,\ 6\nmid i,\\
P-2,&p\nmid k,\ 6\mid i.
\end{cases}
\qquad\text{(13)}
\]

For \(i>j\), it vanishes unless \(6\mid j\) and \(p\nmid k\), in which case it is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.18}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.18}

\[
P^{j-1}(P-1)\chi_p(k)^{i-j}.
\qquad\text{(14)}
\]

For \(j>i\), conjugate and negate the frequency. Indeed dividing the common power fixes one variable when multiplicities differ; equal multiplicities give a character sum over \(\mathbb F_P^\times\setminus\{1\}\).

\textbf{Artificial extension rule.} \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.16}{(12)} need not hold on noncoprime residual pairs. Extend its right side to all individually allowed \(a,b\) and first insert

\hypertarget{cf:eq:A.19}{}

\label{cf:eq:A.19}

\[
\sum_{(a,b)=1}c(a,b)F(Da,Eb;j)
=\sum_t\mu(t)\sum_{t\mid a,b}c(a,b)\widetilde F_{D,E}(a,b;j).
\qquad\text{(15)}
\]

The complete Möbius sum removes artificial pairs before factorwise estimates. Its extra primes are outside genuine common support and inherit none of its valuation restrictions.

\hypertarget{cf:completed-plan}{}

\label{cf:completed-plan}

\hypertarget{the-completed-coefficient-and-the-two-transform-plan}{%
\paragraph{2.3 The completed coefficient and the two-transform plan}\label{the-completed-coefficient-and-the-two-transform-plan}}

For a fixed finite-ray character \(\nu\) set

\hypertarget{cf:completed-definition}{}

\label{cf:completed-definition}

\[
\mathcal C_V(N;k,\nu)=\sum_{c\ {\rm sf},b}
\frac{\overline{\alpha(cb^3)}\gamma_2(c)\nu(cb^3)\chi_{cb^3}(k)}{\sqrt{q_c}q_b}
V(q_cq_b^3/N).
\qquad\text{(16)}
\]

The ideals \(c,b\) may share primes. Exact cube inversion removes the completing \(b\)-sum for the inverse moment. The plain moment instead closes a two-factor family with fixed displayed moving radical: its width includes that entire radical, even at exponent zero. Later marked coefficients must use the appropriate closure.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:B}{}

\label{cf:sec:B}

\hypertarget{cubic-theta-reflection-and-completed-energy}{%
\subsubsection{3 Cubic-theta reflection and completed energy}\label{cubic-theta-reflection-and-completed-energy}}

\hypertarget{cf:sec:B.1}{}

\label{cf:sec:B.1}

\hypertarget{cubic-reflection}{%
\paragraph{3.1 Cubic reflection}\label{cubic-reflection}}

For \(k=u_0k_Sk_{\rm good}\), the unit, \(S\)-valuations modulo six and good row's ray class give finitely many characters \(\nu(A)\chi_A(u_0k_S)R(A,k_{\rm good})\). Remaining dependence is in local good-prime exponents; the reflection's fixed modulus contains no moving prime.

Let \(\nu_0\) lie in a fixed finite set of ray characters with prescribed zeros. At finitely many good primes choose \(j_{\mathfrak p}\in\{0,\ldots,5\}\) and set \(\Psi(A)=\nu_0(A)\prod_{\mathfrak p}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(A)^{j_{\mathfrak p}}\). Define

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.1}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.1}

\[
\mathcal C_V(X;\Psi)=
\sum_{n\ \mathrm{sf},b}
\frac{\gamma_2(n)\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}\Psi(nb^3)}{\sqrt{q_n}\,q_b}
V(q_nq_b^3/X).
\qquad\text{(17)}
\]

\textbf{Proposition 3.1} (Cubic reflection). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:reflection}{}\label{cf:reflection} For annular \(V\), or the Gaussian used in the probe, this sum is a divisor-bounded finite sum of branches \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.2}{}\label{cf:eq:B.2}}

\[
\zeta\sum_{\mu\ne0}\frac{d(\mu)\alpha(\mu)\vartheta(\lambda^4\mu)}{\sqrt{q_\mu}}
\prod_{\mathfrak p\in A}B_{\mathfrak p,j_{\mathfrak p}}(\lambda^4\mu)
V^\sharp\!\left(\frac{q_\mu X}{q_{c_F}^{2}q_r^{2}}\right),
\quad r=\prod_{\mathfrak p\in A}\mathfrak p,
\qquad\text{(18)}
\]

\emph{where \(c_F\) has finitely many fixed possibilities and the local factors are \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.3}{}\label{cf:eq:B.3}}

\[
B_{\mathfrak p,j}(x)=
\begin{cases}
\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^{-j-2},&j\notin\{0,4\},\\
q_{\mathfrak p}^{-1/2}(-1+q_{\mathfrak p}1_{\mathfrak p\mid x}),&j=4,\\
q_{\mathfrak p}^{-1/2}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^{-2},&j=0\text{ active}.
\end{cases}
\qquad\text{(19)}
\]

\emph{Every nonzero exponent is active. An exponent-zero prime additionally has an inactive branch, scalar \(1-q_{\mathfrak p}^{-1}\), omitting it from \(r\).}

\emph{The branch scalar, needed when the prime varies, is \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.4}{}\label{cf:eq:B.4}}

\[
\zeta=-\frac{i}{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\widehat\phi(h_0)
\overline{\kappa_F}
\prod_{\mathfrak p\in I}(1-q_{\mathfrak p}^{-1})
\prod_{\mathfrak p\in A}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(\sigma_{\mathfrak p})^{-2}
\omega_{\mathfrak p,j_{\mathfrak p}},\qquad c=c_Fr,
\qquad\text{(20)}
\]

\emph{Here \(I\) is the inactive set, \(\widehat\phi\) the normalized fixed-modulus Fourier coefficient below, and}

\[
\begin{gathered}
\sigma_{\mathfrak p}=\lambda^2c/\mathfrak p,\qquad
\epsilon_{\mathfrak p}=-\bigl((\lambda^3c/\mathfrak p)\sigma_{\mathfrak p}\bigr)^{-1}
\pmod{\mathfrak p},\\
\omega_{\mathfrak p,j}=
\begin{cases}
\chi_{\mathfrak p}(-1)^j\gamma_j(\mathfrak p)\gamma_{j+2}(\mathfrak p)
\chi_{\mathfrak p}(\epsilon_{\mathfrak p})^{-j-2},&j\notin\{0,4\},\\
\gamma_4(\mathfrak p),&j=4,\\
-\gamma_2(\mathfrak p)\chi_{\mathfrak p}(\epsilon_{\mathfrak p})^{-2},&j=0.
\end{cases}
\end{gathered}
\]

\emph{The inverse is in the residue field at a unit. Each \(|\omega_{\mathfrak p,j}|=1\), hence \(|\zeta|\le1/81\). The three fixed cusp sequences satisfy \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.5}{}\label{cf:eq:B.5}}

\[
\mu=u\lambda^mnb^3,\qquad m\ge-4,
\qquad |d(u\lambda^mnb^3)|\le27\,3^{m/6}\sqrt{q_b}.
\qquad\text{(21)}
\]

\emph{The dual primary ideals \(n,b\) avoid \(\lambda\); \(n\) is squarefree. They may share primes and meet \(S\), without the primal \(S\)-exclusion. Fixing the active radical modulo one fixed ray modulus makes \(d,\vartheta,c_F\) independent of its moving primes; only a bounded scalar and the displayed local characters vary.}

\emph{With \(\widehat V(t)=\int_0^\infty V(x)x^t\,dx/x\), the exact transform is \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.6}{}\label{cf:eq:B.6}}

\[
V^\sharp(y)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(0)}\widehat V(-t)
\prod_{\pm}\frac{\Gamma(1+t\pm1/6)}{\Gamma(1-t\pm1/6)}
\left(\frac{(2\pi)^4y}{27}\right)^{-t}\,dt.
\qquad\text{(22)}
\]

\emph{For \(V(y)=y^{1/2}W(y)\), with annular \(W\), every fixed \(A,j\) has a finite \(J\) such that \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.7}{}\label{cf:eq:B.7}}

\[
|(y\partial_y)^jV^\sharp(y)|
\ll_{A,j}\|W\|_{C^J}\min(y^{1/4},y^{-A}).
\qquad\text{(23)}
\]

\emph{The same bound holds for \(V_G\), with a fixed constant in place of the annular seminorm.}

\emph{Proof.} Use the classical cubic-theta automorphy and cusp expansions of §\protect\hyperlink{cf:classical-inputs}{1.1}. Put \(\check e(z)=e(\lambda z)\) and \(L(t)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\t&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). On \(\Gamma_1(3)=\{g\in\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathcal O):g\equiv I\pmod3\}\), the theta multiplier is \(\kappa(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right))=(c/a)_3\) for \(c\ne0\), otherwise one; extend to \(\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbf Z)\Gamma_1(3)\) with value one on \(\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbf Z)\). The conjugate theta coefficient at \(\lambda^{-3}nb^3\) is \(3^{5/2}|b|\chi_n(\lambda)^{-2}\gamma_2(n)\), its constant term \(3^{5/2}v^{2/3}/2\). At \(I,L(\lambda),L(-\lambda)\) the expansions are

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.8}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.8}

\[
\sigma_Hv^{2/3}+\sum_{\mu\ne0}d_H(\mu)vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\mu|v)\check e(\mu z).
\qquad\text{(24)}
\]

These cusp expansions give \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.5}{(21)}; their twisted reflection below uses no prime asymptotic.

Fix \(L\) containing primary and supplementary conditions and fixed conductors. Set \(\phi(x)=\chi_x(\lambda)^2\nu_0(x)\) with prescribed primary and nonunit zeros, and \(\widehat\phi(h_0)=q_L^{-1}\sum_{x\bmod L}\phi(x)e(-h_0x/L)\). Expand modulo \(L\). At a moving prime,

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.9}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.9}

\[
q^{-1}\sum_{x\bmod\mathfrak p}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^j e(-hx/\mathfrak p)
=\begin{cases}
q^{-1/2}\tau^-_{\mathfrak p,j}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(h)^{-j},&j\ne0, h\ne0,\\
0,&j\ne0, h=0,\\
-q^{-1},&j=0, h\ne0,\\
1-q^{-1},&j=0, h=0,
\end{cases}
\qquad\text{(25)}
\]

Here \(|\tau^-_{\mathfrak p,j}|=1\). An active branch translates by \(z_h=\lambda^2(h_0/L+\sum_{\mathfrak p\mid r}h_{\mathfrak p}/\mathfrak p)\), all \(h_{\mathfrak p}\ne0\).

Reduce \(\lambda^2h_0/L=a_F/c_F\), unit-normalized with \(a_F\equiv1\pmod3\) if \(\lambda\mid c_F\), otherwise \(c_F\equiv1\pmod3\). Then

\[
c=c_Fr,\qquad a=a_Fr+\lambda^2c_F\sum_{\mathfrak p\mid r}(r/\mathfrak p)h_{\mathfrak p},
\qquad z_h=a/c.
\]

Fix \(r\bmod M^2\), \(M=\lambda^{12}L^4\), and lift \(h_{\mathfrak p}\equiv0\pmod{M^2}\). Complete \((a,c)\) to \(g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b_0\\c&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) with \(\delta\equiv a^{-1}\) to the required depth at primes of \(c_F\), \(\delta\equiv0\) at primes of \(M\) outside \(c_F\), and \(\delta\equiv a^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak p}\) for \(\mathfrak p\mid r\). Choose

\[
H=\begin{cases}
I,&3\mid c,\\
L(u_0),\quad u_0\in\{\lambda,-\lambda\},\ u_0\equiv c\pmod3,
&v_\lambda(c)=1,\\
EL(-u_0),\quad u_0\equiv a\pmod3,&(c,\lambda)=1,
\end{cases}
\qquad E=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]

In the last case \(u_0\) ranges over fixed representatives of \(\mathcal O/3\mathcal O\), not merely \(\{\pm\lambda\}\). These congruences give \(gH^{-1}\equiv I\pmod3\); trivial multipliers on \(\operatorname{SL}_2(\mathbf Z)\) and translations by \(\mathbf Z+3\mathcal O\) reduce to the same three cusps.

Multiplier reciprocity gives \(\kappa(gH^{-1})=\kappa_F\prod_{\mathfrak p\mid r}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(a)^2\). The fixed-sector factors are respectively \((c_F/a)_3\), \((-u_0/(a-u_0b_0))_3((c_F/u_0)/a)_3\), \((a/c_F)_3\), independent of the residual column.

The coordinate formula

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.10}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.10}

\[
g^{-1}(a/c+z,v)=
\left(-\frac\delta c-\frac{\bar z}{c^2(|z|^2+v^2)},
\frac v{q_c(|z|^2+v^2)}\right)
\qquad\text{(26)}
\]

shows that \(\partial_{\bar z}\) at zero kills the constant term and equals \(-(cv)^{-2}\partial_{z'}\). For \(x=\lambda^4\mu\), \(f=\lambda^3c_F\), additive CRT gives

\[
\check e(-\delta\mu/c)=\vartheta(x)
\prod_{\mathfrak p\mid r}e(\epsilon_{\mathfrak p}h_{\mathfrak p}^{-1}x/\mathfrak p),
\quad \vartheta(x)=e(-\delta_Fr^{-1}x/f),
\quad\epsilon_{\mathfrak p}\equiv-(\lambda^5c_F^2(r/\mathfrak p)^2)^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak p}.
\]

The conjugate multiplier contributes \(\chi_{\mathfrak p}(h_{\mathfrak p})^{-2}\); substituting \(y=h_{\mathfrak p}^{-1}\) gives exponent \(j+2\) and exactly the Gauss/Ramanujan factors \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.3}{(19)}, with their zeros. Their normalized scalars are \(\omega_{\mathfrak p,j}\) and \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.4}{(20)}, recording the entire active conductor's dependence.

To check the archimedean normalization, define

\[
\mathcal D_\Psi(s)=\sum_{n\ {\rm sf},b}\gamma_2(n)\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}\Psi(nb^3)q_n^{-s}q_b^{1/2-3s}.
\]

Multiply the conjugate theta \(\mu\)-coefficient by \(\phi(\lambda^3\mu)\prod_{\mathfrak p}\chi_{\mathfrak p}(\lambda^3\mu)^{j_{\mathfrak p}}\), obtaining \(\Theta_\Psi\). Differentiation supplies \(2\pi i\overline\mu\), and

\[
\int_0^\infty v^{2s}K_{1/3}(4\pi|\mu|v)\,dv
=\frac{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s+1/3)\Gamma(s+2/3)}{(4\pi|\mu|)^{2s+1}}.
\]

Consequently, initially for \(\Re s>1\),

\hypertarget{cf:direct-mellin-identity}{}

\label{cf:direct-mellin-identity}

\[
\int_0^\infty\partial_{\bar z}\Theta_\Psi(0,v)v^{2s-1}\,dv
=\frac{3^{5/2}}4\left(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\right)^s
\Gamma(s+1/3)\Gamma(s+2/3)\mathcal D_\Psi(s).
\]

Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.10}{(26)} and change height to \(1/(q_cv)\), contributing \(-c^{-2}q_c^{1-2t}\), with \(|c^{-2}q_c|=1\). Division by the direct Mellin factor and \(t\mapsto-t\) give \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.6}{(22)} and scalar \(1/81\). Differentiated cusp expansions decay at both endpoints, so their Mellin integrals are entire. Absolute convergence on the direct/reflected half-planes, Stirling and Phragmén--Lindelöf give intervening polynomial vertical bounds. Rapid test decay permits shifting to \(\Re t=-1/4\) without gamma poles, giving small-\(y\) decay; arbitrary positive lines give rapid large-\(y\) decay. This proves \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.2}{(18)}--\protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.7}{(23)}, including finite seminorm control. \(\square\)

\hypertarget{cf:sec:B.2}{}

\label{cf:sec:B.2}

\hypertarget{unmarked-energy-including-a-frozen-label}{%
\paragraph{3.2 Unmarked energy, including a frozen label}\label{unmarked-energy-including-a-frozen-label}}

We use the quadratic large sieve (Goldmakher--Louvel, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) in the form

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.11}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.11}

\[
\sum_{q_R\le K}^{*}\left|\sum_{q_n\le N}^{*}c_n\chi_R(n)^3\right|^2
\ll_\varepsilon(KN)^\varepsilon(K+N)\sum_n|c_n|^2.
\qquad\text{(27)}
\]

Coefficients are \(R\)-independent. The primary quadratic symbol has primitive conductor \(R\lambda^{e(R)}\), \(e(R)=(q_R-1)/2\pmod2\); primary adjustment contributes that power of the character modulo \(\lambda\). A primary square class modulo four fixes \(e(R)\). Coprime indices in it have primitive product conductor their product and reciprocity factor \(R(\cdot,\cdot)\), verifying the sieve's Hecke-family hypotheses. Finite ray partition and transpose duality give \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.11}{(27)}. Row restrictions decrease its positive sum; column restrictions are zero coefficients.

For a squarefree ideal \(f\), set

\[
\Psi_k(a)=\nu(a)\chi_a(k)\chi_a(f)^4,
\]

and, with \(V(y)=y^{1/2}W(y)\), define

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.12}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.12}

\[
T(X;k,f)=\sum_{n\ \mathrm{sf},b}
\frac{\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi_k(n)
\overline{\alpha(b)}^{3}\Psi_k(b)^3}{\sqrt{q_n}\,q_b}
V(q_nq_b^3/X).
\qquad\text{(28)}
\]

\textbf{Proposition 3.2} (Completed energy with a frozen label). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:completed-energy}{}\label{cf:completed-energy} For every \(\varepsilon>0\), \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.13}{}\label{cf:eq:B.13}}

\[
\sum_{0<q_k\le H}|T(X;k,f)|^2
\ll(HXq_f)^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J}^2
\left(H+\frac{H^2q_f}{X}\right),\qquad H,X\ge1.
\qquad\text{(29)}
\]

\emph{The constants and smooth order are uniform in \(f\).}

\emph{Proof.} Allow an arbitrary ideal \(g\) in place of \(f\) and squarefree rows \(u_0s\). Write \(s=tk_0\), \(t\mid gS\), \(H_0=H/q_t\), \((k_0,gS)=1\). Residual exponent one turns \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.3}{(19)} into \(\chi_{\mathfrak p}(x)^3\). Frozen exponents are \(j_{\mathfrak p}\equiv1_{\mathfrak p\mid t}+4v_{\mathfrak p}(g)\pmod6\), including every collision; absorb fixed primes in the finite sector.

After fixing unit, \(\lambda\)-valuation and a finite ray class, a reflected branch is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.14}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.14}

\[
S_m[A,Y](k_0)=3^{-m/3}\sum_{n\ \mathrm{sf},b}
\frac{A(n,b)\chi_{k_0}(nb)^3}{\sqrt{q_n}\,q_b}
V^\sharp\!\left(\frac{3^mq_nq_b^3H_0^2}{Yq_{k_0}^2}\right),
\qquad\text{(30)}
\]

times a bounded row scalar, with \(k_0\)-independent \(A\), initially \(|A|\le a=1\), \(Y\asymp H_0^2/X\). For \(j=4\) set \(A_n=1_{\mathfrak p\nmid n}A(\mathfrak pn,b)\), \(A_b=1_{\mathfrak p\nmid n}A(n,\mathfrak pb)\). Splitting \(1_{\mathfrak p\mid nb^3}=1_{\mathfrak p\mid n}+1_{\mathfrak p\nmid n,\mathfrak p\mid b}\) gives, for \(q=q_{\mathfrak p}\),

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.15}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.15}

\[
S_m[AB_{\mathfrak p,4},q^2Y]
=-q^{-1/2}S_m[A,q^2Y]
+\chi_{k_0}(\mathfrak p)^3S_m[A_n,qY]
+q^{-1/2}\chi_{k_0}(\mathfrak p)^3S_m[A_b,Y/q].
\qquad\text{(31)}
\]

This preserves squarefreeness and shared primes. The branch updates of \((a^2,Y)\) are \((a^2,q^2Y)\) for \(j\notin\{0,4\}\); \((a^2/q,q^2Y)\) for active \(j=0\); \((a^2/q,q^2Y),(a^2,qY),(a^2/q,Y/q)\) for \(j=4\). Inactive primes increase neither entry. Consequently,

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.16}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.16}

\[
a^2\le1,\qquad a^2Y\ll H_0^2q_t^2q_g/X.
\qquad\text{(32)}
\]

A prime of \(t\) costs at most \(q^2\); a valuation-one prime of \(g\) outside \(t\) has \(j=4\), costing \(q\); higher valuations cost at most \(q^2\le q^{v_{\mathfrak p}(g)}\).

Dual stars impose squarefreeness without \(S\)-exclusion. Split the finitely many squarefree \(S\)-parts of \(n\) into bounded row factors before \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.11}{(27)}. Retain the actual row cutoff in §\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:1.2}{1.3}'s joint profile; on \(q_n\asymp U,q_b\asymp B\), separate into

\[
G_{b,\tau}(k_0)=\sum_{q_n\asymp U}^{*}
\frac{A(n,b)}{\sqrt{q_n}}(q_n/U)^{i\tau}\chi_{k_0}(n)^3.
\]

Their Fourier densities are \(\ll(1+3^mUB^3/Y)^{-A}(1+|\tau|)^{-2}\). Cauchy and \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.11}{(27)} give

\[
\sum_{q_{k_0}\le H_0}^{*}|G_{b,\tau}(k_0)|^2
\ll a^2(H_0U)^\varepsilon(H_0+U).
\]

Weighted Cauchy--Schwarz in \(b\), using \(\sum_{q_b\asymp B}q_b^{-1}\ll1\), now yields

\[
\|S_{m;U,B}\|_2
\ll a\mathcal D^\varepsilon3^{-m/3}(\sqrt{H_0}+\sqrt U)
(1+3^mUB^3/Y)^{-2},
\]

For \(\mathcal D\ge2+H+X+q_g\), use \(\sum_{U,B}(1+UB^3/Z)^{-2}\ll\log^2(2+Z)\) and \(\sum_{U,B}\sqrt U(1+UB^3/Z)^{-2}\ll\sqrt Z\), then sum \(m\ge-4\), obtaining \(\|\mathrm{branch}\|_2^2\ll a^2\mathcal D^\varepsilon(H_0+Y)\). Equation \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.16}{(32)} and divisor-many branches give

\[
\sum_{q_s\le H}^{*}|T(X;u_0s,g)|^2
\ll(HXq_g)^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J}^2
\left(H+H^2q_g/X\right).
\]

Finally write uniquely \(k=u_0sv^2\), \(s\) squarefree, allowing \((s,v)>1\). Multiplicativity with zeros and \(8\equiv2\pmod6\) give \(T(X;u_0sv^2,f)=T(X;u_0s,fv^2)\). The last bound at \((H/q_v^2,g=fv^2)\) has main expression \(q_v^{-2}(H+H^2q_f/X)\). Sum \(\sum_vq_v^{-2}<\infty\) and six units to obtain \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.13}{(29)}. \(\square\)

Taking \(f=1\), with \(V(y)=y^{1/2}W(y)\), gives

\hypertarget{cf:eq:4.4}{}

\label{cf:eq:4.4}

\[
\sum_{0<q_k\le H}|\mathcal C_V(N;k,\nu)|^2\ll(HN)^\varepsilon(H+H^2/N)\|W\|_{C^J}^2.\qquad\text{(33)}
\]

A log-annular partition extends this to \(V_G\), whose seminorms are summable against every fixed exponential; the row-norm triangle sum converges.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:B.3}{}

\label{cf:sec:B.3}

\hypertarget{a-quadraticcubic-norm-for-one-prime}{%
\paragraph{3.3 A quadratic--cubic norm for one prime}\label{a-quadraticcubic-norm-for-one-prime}}

Let \(k,n\) be squarefree, \(q_k\ll K\), \(q_n\asymp N\), \(q_b\asymp B\) with arbitrary \(b\). Rows avoid \(S\); dual indices may meet \(S\setminus\{\lambda\}\). For \(k\)-independent \(\beta(n,b)\) write

\[
b=gt^2,\quad c=(n,g),\quad n=cm,\quad g=ch,
\qquad q_c\asymp C,\quad q_g\asymp G,\quad q_t\asymp T,
\quad B\asymp GT^2.
\]

Here \(g\) is squarefree, \(c,m,h\) are pairwise coprime squarefree, and \(g,t\) may share primes. Define the dyadic quadratic row norm

\[
\mathcal Q_{C,G,T}(\beta)=
\sum_{\substack{k\ {\rm sf},\ (k,S)=1\\q_k\ll K}}
\left|\sum_{\substack{n\ {\rm sf},\ q_n\asymp N,\ q_b\asymp B\\
 b=gt^2,\ g\ {\rm sf},\ q_g\asymp G,\ q_t\asymp T\\
 c=(n,g),\ q_c\asymp C}}
\beta(n,b)\chi_k(nb)^3\right|^2.
\]

All column restrictions are \(k\)-independent. Then

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.17}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.17}

\[
\mathcal Q_{C,G,T}(\beta)
\ll (KNB)^\varepsilon T
\sum_{q_t\asymp T}\sum_{\substack{r\ {\rm sf},\ (r,S)=1\\q_r\ll\min(K,C)}}
\left(\frac K{q_r}+\frac{NG}{C^2}\right)\frac C{q_r}
\sum_{c',m,h}|\beta(rc'm,rc'ht^2)|^2.
\qquad\text{(34)}
\]

The last sum retains the support, \(c=rc'\). Fixed row and coefficient restrictions are allowed.

\emph{Proof.} The zero-preserving identity is

\[
\chi_k(nb)^3=\chi_k(mh)^3\mathbf1_{(k,ct)=1}.
\]

Cauchy over \(O(T)\) choices of \(t\) supplies \(T\). Fixing \(t\), keep \((k,t)=1\) as a row restriction and expand \(1_{(k,c)=1}=\sum_{r\mid(k,c)}\mu(r)\). Divisor Cauchy costs \((KNB)^\varepsilon\). With \(k=rk'\), \(c=rc'\), squarefreeness imposes \((k',r)=(c',r)=1\); no \((k',c')\)-mask is reinserted termwise.

Absorb \(\chi_r(mh)^3\) into column coefficients; split finite squarefree \(S\)-parts of \(m,h\) into bounded row factors. Their residual product is squarefree and good, of norm \(O(NG/C^2)\). Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.11}{(27)} at \(K/q_r\), group by product at divisor cost, and use Cauchy over \(O(C/q_r)\) choices of \(c'\). This proves \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.17}{(34)}; restore \(S\)-parts inside its positive sum. Empty quotients give zero; in nonempty bounded ranges replace scales by \(\max(1,\text{scale})\) at constant cost. \(\square\)

For \(|\beta|\le1\), ideal counting and dyadic summation in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.17}{(34)} give

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.18}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.18}

\[
\sum_k\left|\sum_{n,b}\beta(n,b)\chi_k(nb)^3\right|^2
\ll(KNB)^\varepsilon(K+NB)NB.
\qquad\text{(35)}
\]

Indeed for \(q_r\asymp r_0\), coefficient count times \(C/q_r\) is \(O(NG/r_0^2)\); \(O(r_0)\) choices of \(r\) and both \(T\)-factors give \(NB/r_0\).

The cubic sieve (Heath-Brown, Theorem 2) for squarefree primary Eisenstein ideals and fixed coefficients is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.19}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.19}

\[
\sum_{q_a\le U}^{*}\left|\sum_{q_b\le V}^{*}c_b\chi_a(b)^2\right|^2
 \ll(UV)^\varepsilon\{U+V+(UV)^{2/3}\}\sum_b|c_b|^2.
\qquad\text{(36)}
\]

Rational norms need not be squarefree; reciprocity and conjugation allow either cubic orientation.

\textbf{Lemma 3.3} (One-prime hybrid norm). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:hybrid-norm}{}\label{cf:hybrid-norm} Let \(p\) run over good primes with \(q_p\asymp L\). Suppose \(|a(p)|\le1\) is independent of \(k,n,b\), and \(|\beta(n,b)|\le1\) is independent of \(k,p\). With the other variables as above, \hypertarget{cf:eq:B.20}{}\label{cf:eq:B.20}}

\[
\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_k\left|\sum_p\frac{a(p)}{\sqrt{q_p}}\mathbf1_{p\nmid k}
 \sum_{n,b}\beta(n,b)\chi_k(nb)^3\chi_p(n)^{-2}\mathbf1_{p\nmid b}\right|^2\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\ll(KNBL)^\varepsilon(K+NB)B\{N+L+(NL)^{2/3}\}.
\end{aligned}
\qquad\text{(37)}
\]

\emph{Separate fixed restrictions on the row, prime and dual supports, including ray sectors, are allowed.}

\emph{Proof.} First omit \(1_{p\nmid k}\). Insert the prime sum into \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.17}{(34)}'s column coefficient. Its decomposition gives

\[
\chi_p(rc'm)^{-2}\mathbf1_{p\nmid rc'ht^2}
 =\chi_p(r)^{-2}\chi_p(c'm)^{-2}\mathbf1_{p\nmid ht}.
\]

The character retains zeros at \(r,c',m\). Fix \(r,h,t\) and absorb \(\chi_p(r)^{-2}1_{p\nmid ht}\) into \(a(p)\), whose central squared mass is \(\sum_{q_p\asymp L}q_p^{-1}\ll1\). In the positive sum discard \(|\beta|^2\le1\), group by squarefree \(j=c'm=n/r\) at divisor cost, and apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.19}{(36)}, giving

\[
(KNBL)^\varepsilon\left\{\frac N{q_r}+L+
                 \left(\frac{NL}{q_r}\right)^{2/3}\right\}.
\]

The \(O(G/C)\) choices of \(h\), both \(T\)-factors and \(C/q_r\) give \(B/q_r\); the dyadic \(r\)-count cancels its denominator. Since \(NG/C^2\ll NB\), summing gives \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.20}{(37)} without the mask.

For collisions put \(k=pk'\). Divisor Cauchy and \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.18}{(35)}, fixing \(p\) and absorbing its characters into \(\beta\), give

\[
\ll(KNBL)^\varepsilon\sum_{q_p\asymp L}\frac1{q_p}
       \left(\frac K{q_p}+NB\right)NB
 \ll(KNBL)^\varepsilon(K/L+NB)NB.
\]

This is bounded by \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.20}{(37)}, whose brace is \(\ge N\). Triangle restores the mask. An absent prime uses \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.18}{(35)}, equivalent to \(L=1\). \(\square\)

\hypertarget{cf:sec:B.4}{}

\label{cf:sec:B.4}

\hypertarget{whole-index-compensation-and-the-required-energy}{%
\paragraph{3.4 Whole-index compensation and the required energy}\label{whole-index-compensation-and-the-required-energy}}

Insert \(1_{p\mid nb^3}\) in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.1}{(17)} for a selected good prime \(p\). If \(p\) is already a base local prime, its original zero kills the sum. Otherwise write

\[
\mathbf1_{p\mid nb^3}=1-\chi_p(nb^3)^0
\]

and reflect both terms. Inactive coefficients combine to \(1-(1-q_p^{-1})=q_p^{-1}\), with \(p\) absent from the conductor. The active dual factor is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.21}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.21}

\[
q_p^{-1/2}\chi_p(\lambda^4\mu)^{-2},
\qquad\text{(38)}
\]

with scalar \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.4}{(20)} replacing \(\omega_{p,0}\) by \(-\omega_{p,0}\). Identical fixed Fourier term and active radical give identical cusp data, so subtract before estimating, even when primal squarefree and cube indices share \(p\).

For distinct active \(r,p\), scalar \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.4}{(20)} has pair factor

\[
\chi_r(p)^{2j_r+2}\chi_p(r)^{2j_p+2}
=(p/r)_3^{j_r+j_p+2}.
\]

For a residual row prime \(j_r=1\) and marked prime \(j_p=0\), this equals one. Freeze other active primes, including every \(j=4\) prime. Separately fixing row product \(R\) and \(P=p\) modulo §\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:B.1}{3.1}'s full \(M^2\) fixes cusp and additive data without pair restrictions. The remaining scalar separates into bounded row and prime factors.

For dual index a fixed factor times \(nb^3\), the moving characters are

\[
\chi_R(nb^3)^3=\chi_R(nb)^3,\qquad
\chi_P(nb^3)^{-2}=\chi_P(n)^{-2}\mathbf1_{(P,b)=1}.
\]

Primal row--prime collisions retain \(1_{(P,R)=1}\); frozen collisions give separate row, prime or dual restrictions. Thus Lemma \protect\hyperlink{cf:hybrid-norm}{3.3}'s two independence hypotheses hold. Logarithmic Fourier separation handles joint completed-norm/prime-norm weights with finite weighted seminorms.

For a bounded row-independent coefficient \(a(\mathfrak p)\), define

\hypertarget{cf:eq:4.5}{}

\label{cf:eq:4.5}

\[
\mathcal T(m)=\sum_{q_{\mathfrak p}\asymp Z^{5/33}}a(\mathfrak p)
 \sum_{c\ {\rm sf},n}\frac{\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}\gamma_2(c)\nu(cn^3)\chi_{cn^3}(m)}
 {\sqrt{q_c}\,q_n}\mathbf1_{\mathfrak p\mid cn^3}
 V\!\left(\frac{q_cq_n^3}{Zq_{\mathfrak p}}\right).
\qquad\text{(39)}
\]

Including a common joint norm profile, the estimate is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:4.6}{}

\label{cf:eq:4.6}

\[
\sum_{0<q_m\ll Z^{28/33}}|\mathcal T(m)|^2\ll Z^{28/33+\varepsilon}.
\qquad\text{(40)}
\]

The prime stays inside the norm throughout the proof.

\textbf{Proposition 3.4} (Whole-index one-prime energy). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:one-prime-energy}{}\label{cf:one-prime-energy} The sum \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:4.5}{(39)} satisfies \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:4.6}{(40)}.}

\emph{Proof.} Locally put \(M=28/33\), \(z=5/33\), \(N_*=1+z=38/33\). Freeze the row's maximal powerful part, \(S\)-supported valuation-one part and unit. Their norm dyad has exponent \(O\), the remaining squarefree row exponent \(H\le M-O+O(1/\log Z)\). Apply §\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:B.1}{3.1}'s finite sector reduction.

For active frozen good primes, let \(A_0\) be radical exponent; \(S_0\) count small Ramanujan and active zero-mask factors; \(N_0,B_0\) count forced squarefree/cube factors. Splitting as in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.15}{(31)} leaves shared cube occurrences in \(b\) on the squarefree branch, or extracts one cube occurrence with factor \(q_p^{-1/2}\). Powerful support gives

\[
2A_0\le O,\qquad 0\le N_0\le A_0,\qquad S_0,B_0\ge0.
\]

Inactive marked primes have mass \(\sum_{q_p\asymp Z^z}q_p^{-1}\ll Z^\varepsilon\); remove by triangle and set \(z_a=0\). Active primes stay inside the norm with \(z_a=z\).

For residual dual squarefree, cube and ramified exponents \(v,b,e_\lambda\), the central coefficient and dual length are

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.22}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.22}

\[
Z^{-v/2-b-e_\lambda/3-(S_0+B_0+z_a)/2},\qquad
 T_d=2H+2A_0+2z_a-N_*-N_0-3B_0.
\qquad\text{(41)}
\]

The actual kernel argument is a fixed factor times \(Z^{v+3b+e_\lambda-T_d}y_ny_b^3y_R^{-2}y_P^{-2}\), each \(y\) normalized by its dyadic center, omitting \(y_P\) when inactive. By \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.7}{(23)}, extract \(Z^{-(T_d-v-3b-e_\lambda)_+/4}\) from the \textbf{linear} vector before Fourier separation; the normalized profile has bounded seminorms. Then enlarge the positive row norm to \(q_R\ll Z^H\).

Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.20}{(37)} at \((K,N,B,L)=(Z^H,Z^v,Z^b,Z^{z_a})\), or \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.18}{(35)} when inactive. Its prime inverse square root is already included. Multiply the remaining squared coefficient and count powerful rows; with \(u_h=\min\{v,z_a,(v+z_a)/3\}\) the exponent is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.23}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.23}

\[
\begin{aligned}
 E={}&O/2+\max(H,v+b)-S_0-B_0+z_a-u_h-b-2e_\lambda/3\\
    &-\tfrac12(T_d-v-3b-e_\lambda)_+.
\end{aligned}
\qquad\text{(42)}
\]

Powerful ideals number \(O(Z^{O/2})\): write \(x^2y^3\), \(y\) squarefree, and sum \(Z^{O/2}q_y^{-3/2}\). Fixed-supported parts and allocations have divisor-many choices. Their Cauchy cost is the single \(O/2+\varepsilon\) already in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.23}{(42)}.

Retain \(v+3b+e_\lambda\le T_d\), suppressing tiny buffers. Since

\[
T_d-H\le M+2z-N_*=0,
\]

we have \(\max(H,v+b)=H\). If \(u_h=z_a\), then \(E\le M\); otherwise \(u_h\ge v/2\). Put \(y_d=v+3b+e_\lambda\); then

\[
u_h+b+2e_\lambda/3+\tfrac12(T_d-y_d)_+
 \ge y_d/4+\tfrac12(T_d-y_d)_+\ge T_d/4.
\]

Substitution in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.23}{(42)}, with \(\Delta_H=M-O-H\ge0\), gives

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.24}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.24}

\[
4(E-M)\le N_*+2z_a-2M-2\Delta_H-(2A_0-N_0+B_0+4S_0)
 \le N_*+2z-2M=-\frac8{33}.
\qquad\text{(43)}
\]

Hence all retained blocks have \(E\le M\).

Discard whole tail dyads outside a small retention buffer before separation; their kernel argument exceeds a positive power of \(Z\). For \(|K(y)|\le C_Ay^{-A}\), \(A>1\), lattice shells give

\hypertarget{cf:eq:B.25}{}

\label{cf:eq:B.25}

\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne\mu\in\lambda^{-4}\mathcal O\\a q_\mu>U}}
 |K(aq_\mu)|\ll_A C_A(1+a^{-1})U^{1-A},\qquad U\ge1.
\qquad\text{(44)}
\]

Indeed each dyadic shell has \(O(1+2^jU/a)\) points. Raw coefficients, rows, primes and branches have fixed polynomial cost; choose \(A\) afterwards for an arbitrarily small tail. The lower bound \(e_\lambda\ge-4\log_Z3\) and fixed support shifts cost \(O(1/\log Z)\). Buffers, dyads and finite profile orders fit every requested loss, proving \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:4.6}{(40)}. \(\square\)

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C}{}

\label{cf:sec:C}

\hypertarget{the-inverse-second-moment}{%
\subsubsection{4 The inverse second moment}\label{the-inverse-second-moment}}

The two Poisson transforms recover the essential Möbius coefficients through the cubic Gauss identities.

\textbf{Theorem 4.1} (Inverse second moment). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:inverse-moment}{}\label{cf:inverse-moment} Fix \(C,\kappa,\varepsilon>0\). For \(Z\ge2\), \(1\le D,H\le Z^C\), and \(H\ge DZ^\kappa\), \hypertarget{cf:eq:C.1}{}\label{cf:eq:C.1}}

\[
\sum_{0<q_u\le H}\left|D^{-1/2}
\sum_n\mu(n)\nu(n)\chi_n(u)^{\pm1}W(q_n/D)\right|^2
\ll HZ^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J}^2.
\qquad\text{(45)}
\]

\emph{The fixed \(\nu\) includes its presentation's Euler deletions. The order depends only on \(C,\kappa,\varepsilon\), support and fixed arithmetic data; test norm twists cost a fixed height power.}

\emph{Proof.} We prove it through the positive energy

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.2}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.2}

\[
E(H,X,F;\xi,W)=\frac1{XF}
\sum_{F\le q_f<2F}^{*}\sum_{0<q_k\le H}
\left|\sum_n^{*}a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(q_n/X)\right|^2.
\qquad\text{(46)}
\]

The label \(f\) is averaged. Write \(E^{(r)}\) for an added inner mask \(1_{(n,r)=1}\); \(T\) is \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.12}{(28)}.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C.1}{}

\label{cf:sec:C.1}

\hypertarget{poisson-smooth-separation-and-punctures}{%
\paragraph{4.1 Poisson, smooth separation, and punctures}\label{poisson-smooth-separation-and-punctures}}

For a primitive character \(\psi\) modulo \(m\), masked Poisson summation is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.3}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.3}

\[
\sum_{k\in\mathcal O}\psi(k)1_{(k,r)=1}\Phi(q_k/H)
=\frac{H\gamma(\psi)}{\sqrt{q_m}}
\sum_{d\mid\operatorname{rad}r}\frac{\mu(d)\psi(d)}{q_d}
\sum_h\overline{\psi(h)}\widehat\Phi\!\left(\frac{Hq_h}{q_dq_m}\right).
\qquad\text{(47)}
\]

Here \(\Phi\) is radial Schwartz and \(\gamma(\psi)=q_m^{-1/2}\sum_{x\bmod m}\psi(x)e(x/m)\). Expand the mask as \(\sum_{d\mid(r,k)}\mu(d)\), substitute \(k=d\ell\), and Poisson-sum modulo \(m\). The finite transform is \(\sqrt{q_m}\gamma(\psi)\overline{\psi(h)}\), with nonunit zeros. Primitive principal modulus one has value one at \(h=0\), contributing \(H\widehat\Phi(0)\prod_{\mathfrak p\mid r}(1-q_{\mathfrak p}^{-1})\). Shared primes of \(m,r\) vanish through \(\psi(d)\).

Smooth quadratic kernels are separated by \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:1.4}{(5)}.

Möbius inversion and \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.1}{(6)} remove a puncture without changing \(XF\):

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.4}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.4}

\[
E^{(r)}(H,X,F;\xi,W)
\le\tau(\operatorname{rad}r)
\sum_{d\mid\operatorname{rad}r}E(H,X/q_d,Fq_d;\xi,W).
\qquad\text{(48)}
\]

Set \(n=dm\), changing \(f\) to \(df\); \(\chi_m(d)^4\) retains \((m,d)=1\), and \((d,f)>1\) vanishes. Cauchy in \(d\) and positive enlargement of the injective image \(f\mapsto df\) give \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.4}{(48)}, preserving \(XF\).

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C.2}{}

\label{cf:sec:C.2}

\hypertarget{canonical-estimate-and-cube-completion}{%
\paragraph{4.2 Canonical estimate and cube completion}\label{canonical-estimate-and-cube-completion}}

We claim that, for every fixed \(C_0\ge1,\kappa>0\),

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.5}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.5}

\[
E(H,X,F;\xi,W)\ll Z^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J}^2,
\quad \Sigma=XF\le Z^{C_0},\quad
H,X,F\ge1,\quad H\le\Sigma Z^{-\kappa}.
\qquad\text{(49)}
\]

The derivative order is independent of the location of the fixed support interval.

For \(\Psi_{k,f}(n)=\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4\), cube inversion gives the exact identity

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.6}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.6}

\[
X^{-1/2}\sum_n^{*}a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(q_n/X)
=\sum_h\frac{\mu(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^{3}\Psi_{k,f}(h)^3}{q_h}
T(X/q_h^3;k,f).
\qquad\text{(50)}
\]

Opening \(T\), each total cube \(b\) has divisor factor \(\sum_{h\mid b}\mu(h)=1_{b=1}\), by complete multiplicativity with zeros.

Put \(H_c^3=\min(X,X^2/H^2)\), \(L_b=X/q_b^3\). Weighted Cauchy, \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:B.13}{(29)} and the \(f\)-average bound \(q_h\le H_c\) by

\[
Z^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J}^2\left(H+\frac{H^2F H_c^3}{X}\right)
\ll Z^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J}^2\quad(H\le\Sigma).
\]

Reopen the remaining \(T\) terms. A total cube \(b\) has coefficient \(\overline{\alpha(b)}^3\xi(b)^3 1_{(b,f)=1}\sum_{h\mid b,q_h>H_c}\mu(h)\), bounded by \(\tau(b)\), and bounded row multiplier \(\chi_b(k)^3\). A second weighted Cauchy uses \(\sum_{q_b\le R}\tau(b)/q_b\ll\log^2(2R)\) to give

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.7}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.7}

\[
E(H,X,F;\xi,W)
\ll Z^\varepsilon\left\{\Sigma\|W\|_{C^J}^2+
\sup_{q_b>H_c,\ L_b>1}E(H,L_b,F;\xi,W)\right\}.
\qquad\text{(51)}
\]

For \(L_b\le1\), nonempty annular support has boundedly many columns, costing \(O(H)\). The supremum is empty when \(H^2\le X\).

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C.3}{}

\label{cf:sec:C.3}

\hypertarget{two-poisson-transformations-and-the-local-projector}{%
\paragraph{4.3 Two Poisson transformations and the local projector}\label{two-poisson-transformations-and-the-local-projector}}

For \(1\le H,L,F,\Sigma\le Z^{C_0}\) and \(\max(H,LF)\le\Sigma\), we prove

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.8}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.8}

\[
E(H,L,F;\xi,W)
\ll Z^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{4m+12}}^2
\left(1+\sup\frac{E(H',X',F';\xi',U)}{\Sigma'}\right),
\qquad\text{(52)}
\]

where \(\Sigma'=X'F'\), \(H',X',F'\ge1\), \(\|U\|_{C^m}\le1\) on a fixed enlargement of the support, and

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.9}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.9}

\[
H'\le\frac{HL}{\Sigma F},\qquad
\frac{H'}{\Sigma'}\le\frac H\Sigma,\qquad \Sigma'\le L.
\qquad\text{(53)}
\]

Square a rescaled real Schwartz function of compact Fourier support to obtain radial \(\Phi\ge1\) on \([0,1]\), \(\Phi\ge0\), with \(\operatorname{supp}\widehat\Phi\subset[0,C_\Phi]\). For \(\operatorname{supp}W\subset[u,v]\), fix \(c_I\ge\max(1,4C_\Phi v^2)\) and enlarged support \([u/2,2v]\).

Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.3}{(47)} to the square. Write \(n_i=Cu_i\), \(C=(n_1,n_2)\); the primitive row conductor is \(u_1u_2\), residual mask \(C\), divisor \(d\mid C\). Zero frequency costs \(O(H\|W\|_\infty^2)\). Use \(a_\xi(n)\gamma_1(n)=\mu(n)G(n)\xi(n)\), expand its fixed ray factor, and remove \((u_1,u_2)=1\) by complete Möbius inversion, \(u_i=tx_i\). A fixed ray component is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.10}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.10}

\[
P_{C,d,t}(y;U)=\sum_n^{*}\mu(n)\xi_1(n)1_{(n,t)=1}
\overline{\chi_n(y)}\chi_n(C)^4\chi_n(d)U(q_n/\ell),
\qquad \ell=\frac L{q_Cq_t}.
\qquad\text{(54)}
\]

Here \(C,t\) are coprime squarefree, \(d\mid C\), \(q_Cq_t\le2vL\). Combine frequency and label as \(y=hf^2\), retaining \((f,Ct)=(h,t)=1\). This map has divisor-bounded fibres; enlarge its nonzero image to \(q_y\le Y_{C,d}\), where

\[
w_{C,d}=\frac{Hq_C}{q_dL^2F},\qquad
Y_{C,d}=\frac{c_I\Sigma LFq_d}{Hq_C^2}.
\]

Since \(\Sigma\ge LF\), Cauchy and smooth separation give the positive form

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.11}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.11}

\[
Q_{\xi_1}(U)=
\sum_{\substack{C,t\ \mathrm{sf}\\(C,t)=1\\q_Cq_t\le2vL}}
\sum_{d\mid C}w_{C,d}\sum_y\Phi(q_y/Y_{C,d})|P_{C,d,t}(y;U)|^2.
\qquad\text{(55)}
\]

If \(Q_{\xi_1}(U)\le M\|U\|_{C^j}^2\) for all common \(U\), then \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:1.4}{(5)} gives \(E\ll Z^\varepsilon(\Sigma+M)\|W\|_{C^{2j+4}}^2\). Kernels combine annular weights, inverse roots and \(\widehat\Phi(A/(x_1x_2))\) on bounded support, with uniform derivatives.

Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.3}{(47)} to \(y\), write columns \(gz_i\), take \(e\mid g\), and remove \((z_1,z_2)=1\) by \(z_i=wn_i\). Nonzero terms have

\[
(g,Ct)=(w,gCth)=1,\qquad e\mid g,\qquad h\ne0,
\]

and sign \(\mu(e)\mu(w)\). Inverse conversion \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.1}{(6)} restores cubic coefficients. The ray phase is \(\xi_1(z_1z_2^{-1})G(z_2z_1^{-1})\); expanding \(\xi_1(z)G(z^{-1})=\sum_{\xi'}c_{\xi'}\xi'(z)\) gives \(a_{\xi'}(n)\) independent of reconstruction choices.

Reconstruct the puncture, label and row by

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.12}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.12}

\[
r=tg/e,\qquad f'=Cew,\qquad k'=deh,
\qquad a^\sharp(n)=a_{\xi'}(n)1_{(n,r)=1}\chi_n(k')\chi_n(f')^4.
\qquad\text{(56)}
\]

The ideals \(r,f'\) are coprime squarefree. Locally,

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.13}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.13}

\[
\chi_n(dh)\overline{\chi_n(e)}\chi_n(C)^4
=\chi_n(deh)\chi_n(Ce)^4;
\qquad\text{(57)}
\]

Both sides have unit exponent five at \(e\) and vanish at nonunits; \(w\) contributes \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.1}{(6)}'s multiplicative correction.

For \(U_0(x)=x^{-1/2}U(x)\), the scale, scalar and kernel are

\[
X'_0=\frac\ell{q_gq_w}=\frac L{q_rq_{f'}},
\qquad w_{C,d}\frac{Y_{C,d}q_g}{q_e\ell}=\frac{c_I\Sigma q_r}{L^2},
\]

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.14}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.14}

\[
K_{r,f',k'}(x_1,x_2)=U_0(x_1)\overline{U_0(x_2)}
\widehat\Phi\!\left(\frac{c_I\Sigma Fq_{k'}q_r^2}{HLx_1x_2}\right).
\qquad\text{(58)}
\]

These depend only on \((r,f',k')\). Support forces \(q_rq_{f'}\le2vL\) and \(0<q_{k'}\le HL/(\Sigma Fq_r^2)\).

At fixed \((r,f',k')\), all preimages are

\[
t\mid r,\quad f'=Cew,\quad e\mid k',\quad(w,k')=1,
\quad d\mid(C,k'),\quad g=e(r/t),\quad h=k'/(de),
\]

with disjoint \(f'=Cew\). For \(\mathfrak p\mid f'\), write \(I_{\mathfrak p}=1_{\mathfrak p\mid k'}\); allocation to \(C,e,w\) gives

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.15}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.15}

\[
\underbrace{(1+I_{\mathfrak p})}_{\mathfrak p\mid C}
+\underbrace{(-I_{\mathfrak p})}_{\mathfrak p\mid e}
+\underbrace{(-(1-I_{\mathfrak p}))}_{\mathfrak p\mid w}
=I_{\mathfrak p}.
\qquad\text{(59)}
\]

The \(C\) terms distinguish \(\mathfrak p\nmid d\) and \(\mathfrak p\mid d\). Shared kernel, scalar and character permit this signed cancellation before absolute values: it forces \(f'\mid k'\), while \(t\mid r\) contributes \(\tau(r)\).

Writing \(Z_Q\) for the second zero frequency, we have

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.16}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.16}

\[
Q_{\xi_1}(U)-Z_Q=
\frac{c_I\Sigma}{L^2}\sum_{\xi'}c_{\xi'}
\sum_{\substack{r,f'\ \mathrm{sf}\\(r,f')=1}}q_r\tau(r)
\sum_{\substack{k'\ne0\\f'\mid k'}}\sum_{n_1,n_2}^{*}
a^\sharp(n_1)\overline{a^\sharp(n_2)}
K_{r,f',k'}(q_{n_1}/X'_0,q_{n_2}/X'_0).
\qquad\text{(60)}
\]

By \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.3}{(47)}, \(|Z_Q|\ll Z^\varepsilon\Sigma\|U\|_\infty^2\), since its absolute divisor sum is \(\ll\Sigma\sum_C\tau(C)q_C^{-2}\sum_{q_t\ll L}q_t^{-1}\).

On dyads \(q_r\asymp R,q_{f'}\asymp F'\), set

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.17}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.17}

\[
X'=\frac L{RF'},\qquad \Sigma'=\frac LR,
\qquad H'=\frac{HL}{\Sigma FR^2}.
\qquad\text{(61)}
\]

Nonempty support and the projector give \(F'\le q_{k'}\le HL/(\Sigma Fq_r^2)\), hence \(H'\ge1\). For \(j\mid r\),

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.18}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.18}

\[
\frac{X'}{q_j}\ge\frac{\Sigma Fq_r^2}{HRq_j}
\ge\frac{\Sigma F}{H}\ge1.
\qquad\text{(62)}
\]

Puncture removal therefore preserves admissibility and \(\Sigma'\). Since \(1\le X'/X'_0<4\), rescaled kernels have fixed enlarged support and uniform \(C^{2m+4}\) norm \(\ll\|U\|_{C^{2m+4}}^2\).

Writing \(S_m\) for \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.8}{(52)}'s supremum, remove punctures and positively enlarge \((f',k')\) to bound each retained \(r\)'s common-test sum by \(Z^\varepsilon(\Sigma')^2S_m\|V\|_{C^m}^2\). Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:1.4}{(5)} and count \(O(R)\) values of \(r\); the scalar is

\[
\frac{\Sigma R}{L^2}\,R(\Sigma')^2=\Sigma.
\]

Absorb divisor and dyadic counts in \(Z^\varepsilon\). This proves \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.8}{(52)}; \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.17}{(61)} gives all inequalities \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.9}{(53)}.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C.4}{}

\label{cf:sec:C.4}

\hypertarget{closing-the-finite-depth-induction}{%
\paragraph{4.4 Closing the finite-depth induction}\label{closing-the-finite-depth-induction}}

Inductively prove \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.5}{(49)} for \(H\le Z^{j\kappa}\). At \(j=0\), counting gives \(E\ll HX\|W\|_\infty^2\le\Sigma\|W\|_\infty^2\). Next use \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.7}{(51)}. A nonempty supremum has \(H^2>X\), \(H_c^3=X^2/H^2\), \(q_b>H_c\). Apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.8}{(52)} at \(L=L_b\), keeping \(\Sigma=XF\); every child satisfies

\[
H'\le\frac{HL_b}{\Sigma F}=\frac H{q_b^3F^2}
<H\left(\frac H\Sigma\right)^2
\le Z^{-2\kappa}H\le Z^{j\kappa},
\quad H'/\Sigma'\le Z^{-\kappa},\quad \Sigma'\le\Sigma.
\]

Induction applies. Distribute losses among completion, transfer and child, propagating orders backwards by \(m\mapsto4m+12\) and retaining the completed-energy order. Depth \(\le\lceil C_0/\kappa\rceil\) gives finite orders and support enlargements, fixed before external heights. This proves \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.5}{(49)}.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:C.5}{}

\label{cf:sec:C.5}

\hypertarget{return-to-the-inverse-polynomial}{%
\paragraph{4.5 Return to the inverse polynomial}\label{return-to-the-inverse-polynomial}}

Majorize the \(u\)-ball by \(\Phi(q_u/H)\), expand and apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.3}{(47)}. Write columns \(gz_i\), mask divisor \(e\mid g\), frequency \(h\ne0\); zero frequency costs \(O(H\|W\|_\infty^2)\). Inverse conversion \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.1}{(6)} and fixed ray expansion restore cubic coefficients. Remove \((z_1,z_2)=1\) by \(z_i=vn_i\). Retaining zeros, the bijection is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.19}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.19}

\[
b=g/e,\qquad f=ev,\qquad k=eh;
\qquad e=(f,k),\quad v=f/e,\quad g=be,\quad h=k/e.
\qquad\text{(63)}
\]

Here \(b,f\) are coprime squarefree. The identities \(gv=bf\), \(he^5v^4=kf^4\), \(\mu(e)\mu(v)=\mu(f)\) and \(q_g/q_e=q_b\) give each nonzero ray component

\hypertarget{cf:eq:C.20}{}

\label{cf:eq:C.20}

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac H{D^2}\sum_{\substack{b,f\ \mathrm{sf}\\(b,f)=1}}\mu(f)q_b
\sum_{k\ne0}\sum_{n_1,n_2}^{*}
&a_\xi(n_1)\overline{a_\xi(n_2)}
\chi_{n_1}(kf^4)\overline{\chi_{n_2}(kf^4)}1_{(n_1n_2,b)=1}\\
&\times W_0(q_{bfn_1}/D)\overline{W_0(q_{bfn_2}/D)}
\widehat\Phi\!\left(\frac{Hq_k}{q_f^2q_{n_1}q_{n_2}}\right),
\end{aligned}
\qquad\text{(64)}
\]

with \(W_0(x)=x^{-1/2}W(x)\) and support \(q_bq_f\ll D\), \(q_k\ll D^2/(Hq_b^2)\). On \(q_b\asymp B,q_f\asymp F\) put

\[
X=\frac D{BF},\qquad \mathcal H=\frac{C D^2}{HB^2},\qquad \Sigma=XF=\frac DB,
\]

Fixed \(C\) covers supports. Since \(\mathcal H/\Sigma=CD/(HB)\ll Z^{-\kappa}\), apply \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.5}{(49)} with gap \(\kappa/2\), then \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:1.4}{(5)}, \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.4}{(48)}. Kernels have uniform derivatives, giving dyad bound

\[
Z^\varepsilon\frac{HB}{D^2}\,B(XF)^2\|W\|_{C^J}^2
=HZ^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J}^2.
\]

Subunit column scales are empty or have boundedly many columns; count them. Subunit row scales are empty. Sum dyads and fixed ray components, including zero frequency, to prove \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:C.1}{(45)}. Conjugation with \(\nu\mapsto\bar\nu\) gives the other orientation. Finite seminorms of \(W(y)y^{it}\) cost \((1+|t|)^{O(1)}\).

For \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:3.2}{(95)}, take \(Z=\max(2,D,H)\). If \(D\ge2\) and \(H\ge D^{1+\vartheta}\), choose \(\kappa=\vartheta/(1+\vartheta)\); rename the loss to get \(H(HD)^\varepsilon\). Count bounded columns. All element rows, both orientations and common-profile control follow. \(\square\)

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D}{}

\label{cf:sec:D}

\hypertarget{the-plain-moment-with-moving-radical}{%
\subsubsection{5 The plain moment with moving radical}\label{the-plain-moment-with-moving-radical}}

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.1}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.1}

\hypertarget{equal-product-rectangle-cancellation}{%
\paragraph{5.1 Equal-product rectangle cancellation}\label{equal-product-rectangle-cancellation}}

Two Poisson transforms decrease width except for fixed-character rows; equal-product rectangle subtraction cancels their principal volume.

Fix a finite group \(\Theta\) of \(S\)-supported ray characters closed under reciprocity, conjugation and \(n\mapsto\chi_n(-1)\). For \(\vartheta\in\Theta\), squarefree \(q_{R_*}\le Z^B\) and fixed-annular \(W_1,W_2\), put

\[
L_i(X,t)=\sum_n\vartheta(n)\mathbf1_{(n,R_*)=1}q_n^{it}W_i(q_n/X).
\]

Poisson summation gives, uniformly for \(X>0\),

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.1}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.1}

\[
L_i(X,t)=c_{\vartheta,R_*}X^{1+it}I_i(t)
 +O\bigl(Z^\epsilon(1+|t|)^J\bigr),\qquad |L_i(X,t)|\ll X,
\qquad\text{(65)}
\]

Here \(I_i(t)\) is a fixed lattice-measure multiple of \(\int W_i(y)y^{it}\,dy\), and \(c_{\vartheta,R_*}\) is \(X,t\)-independent.

Without the mask, Poisson on fixed lattice cosets for \(X^{it}f_t(z/\sqrt X)\) gives the zero-frequency main term. Integration by parts bounds other frequencies by \((1+|t|)^J(1+|\xi|)^{-J}\); \(J>4\) handles \(X\ge1\), counting \(X<1\). Insert

\[
L_i(X,t)=\sum_{d\mid R_*}\mu(d)\vartheta(d)q_d^{it}L_i^0(X/q_d,t).
\]

The main coefficient is

\[
c_{\vartheta,R_*}=c_\vartheta\sum_{d\mid R_*}\frac{\mu(d)\vartheta(d)}{q_d}.
\]

Norm powers cancel in this coefficient; divisor-many errors cost \(Z^\epsilon\). Counting gives \(|L_i|\ll X\).

Consequently, if

\[
U_1U_2=V_1V_2=T,\qquad U_i,V_i\ge Z^r\quad(i=1,2),\quad r\ge0,
\]

then

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.2}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.2}

\[
T^{-1/2}\left|L_1(U_1,t)L_2(U_2,t)-L_1(V_1,t)L_2(V_2,t)\right|
\ll Z^\epsilon(1+|t|)^{2J}T^{1/2}Z^{-r}.
\qquad\text{(66)}
\]

Main terms equal \(c_{\vartheta,R_*}^2T^{1+it}I_1(t)I_2(t)\). Error scales are \(\le TZ^{-r}\); the error product also fits since \(T\ge Z^{2r}\). Without a positive lower scale, use volume \(O(T^{1/2})\).

The character, mask and norm power must agree across both factors and rectangles.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.2}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.2}

\hypertarget{the-family-closed-under-both-transforms}{%
\paragraph{5.2 The family closed under both transforms}\label{the-family-closed-under-both-transforms}}

Moving twists and a common exclusion close the family under both transforms; no selected prime or zero-free hypothesis is used.

Fix bounded nonnegative real ranges for \(m,q,n_1,n_2,B\). A fixed twist within a row sum has the form

\[
\tau(n)=\nu(n)\prod_{p\mid A}\chi_n(p)^{e_p},\qquad \nu\in\Theta,\quad q_{\operatorname{rad}A}\le Z^q,
\]

Retain all displayed zeros, including exponents \(0\bmod6\), and count their moving support in \(q\). Reciprocity reduces other residue products to this form. A squarefree puncture \(q_R\le Z^B\) is fixed across the row sum and all plain factors. Define

\[
\psi_k(n)=\tau(n)\chi_n(k)\mathbf1_{(n,R)=1},\qquad
S_k(N;W)=N^{-1/2}\sum_n\psi_k(n)W(q_n/N).
\]

\textbf{Theorem 5.1} (Moving-radical mixed plain moment). \emph{\hypertarget{cf:mixed-plain-moment}{}\label{cf:mixed-plain-moment} For every fixed \(\epsilon>0\),}

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.3}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.3}

\[
\sum_{\substack{0<q_k\le Z^m\\\psi_k^*\ne1}}
 |S_k(Z^{n_1};W_1)S_k(Z^{n_2};W_2)|^2
\ll Z^{m+q+\epsilon}.
\qquad\text{(67)}
\]

\emph{No conductor lower bound or condition on \(n_1+n_2\) is imposed. The bound carries \(\|W_1\|_{C^J}^2\|W_2\|_{C^J}^2\), uniformly over moving supports, punctures and bounded lengths, with §\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:1.2}{1.3}'s real-power and height control. Increase \(J\) for any fixed list of Euler/profile derivatives. With no moving twist and two copies of one polynomial,}

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.4}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.4}

\[
\sum_{\substack{0<q_k\le H\\(\nu\chi_\bullet(k))^*\ne1}}
 |S_k(N;W)|^4\ll H^{1+\epsilon}
\qquad\text{(68)}
\]

\emph{for bounded polynomial ranges of \(N\) relative to \(H\).}

\emph{Proof.} Descend in effective width \(M=m+q\); fixed-conductor twists alone do not close under the first transform.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.3}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.3}

\hypertarget{reflection-and-the-centered-comparison}{%
\paragraph{5.3 Reflection and the centered comparison}\label{reflection-and-the-centered-comparison}}

A natural presentation has exactly fixed, displayed moving and row zeros. Remove extra punctures by

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.5}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.5}

\[
S_{k,R}(N;W)=\sum_{d\mid R}\mu(d)\psi_k^0(d)q_d^{-1/2}S_k^0(N/q_d;W).
\qquad\text{(69)}
\]

Its coefficient mass is \(Z^\epsilon\). At each width prove all natural lengths before removing extra punctures; recursive children have smaller width.

Let \(\psi_k^*\) have conductor norm \(Q_k\), and put \(C_k=3Q_k/(2\pi)^2\). If \(R_{0,k}\) is the redundant natural radical, then

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.6}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.6}

\[
C_kq_{R_{0,k}}\ll Z^M.
\qquad\text{(70)}
\]

Each good prime is counted once in primitive conductor or redundant radical, whose union lies in row radical and displayed moving support. Fixed primes cost a constant.

The primitive functional equation gives exactly

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.7}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.7}

\[
S_{\psi_k^*}(N;W)=\epsilon_k S_{\overline{\psi_k^*}}(C_k/N;W^\sharp),\qquad
\mathcal MW^\sharp(s)=\mathcal MW(1-s)\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma(1-s)}.
\qquad\text{(71)}
\]

Central normalization adds no \(C_k,N\) power. Inside an absolute product, conjugate the entire reflected factor and profile to restore orientation. Delete redundant Euler factors before reflection, restore geometrically afterwards: scales are \(C_kq_d/(Nq_h)\), \(d\mid R_{0,k}\), \(\operatorname{rad}h\mid R_{0,k}\), with coefficients \(\epsilon_k\mu(d)\psi_k^*(d)\overline{\psi_k^*(h)}/\sqrt{q_dq_h}\). Their mass is \(Z^\epsilon\) and largest scale \(\ll Z^M/N\).

The gamma quotient gives bounded Euler derivatives at zero and rapid decay at infinity, hence a centrally summable annular partition with polynomial height cost. With padding \(\xi>0\), retain dual lengths \(\le M-\log_ZN+\xi\) and arbitrarily small tails. Apply §\protect\hyperlink{cf:sec:1.2}{1.3}'s Sobolev common-profile bounds to rowwise scales.

Reflect any original factor longer than \(M/2\). It remains to prove a padded core with

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.8}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.8}

\[
n_i\le M/2+\xi,\qquad A:=n_1+n_2\le M+2\xi.
\qquad\text{(72)}
\]

At each width first prove the uncentered range \(A\le5M/6\). Otherwise put \(L=M/4\). If one factor is shorter than \(L\), reflect the other: total becomes \(\le3M/2-A+\xi<2M/3+\xi\), already covered. If both are \(\ge L\), compare

\[
S_k(X_1;W_1)S_k(X_2;W_2)
\quad\hbox{with}\quad
S_k(Y_1;W_1)S_k(Y_2;W_2),
\]

where \(Y_1=Z^L\), \(Y_2=X_1X_2/Y_1\). Reflecting the latter's second factor again gives total \(\le3M/2-A+\xi\), so the comparison is known at this width. Retain the difference as one coefficient; its positive squared norm may include all rows. Subtraction controls added principal rows.

For fixed extracted ideals \(b_1,b_2\), the coefficient to preserve is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.9}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.9}

\[
\mathcal D_b(l_1,l_2)=
W_1(q_{b_1}q_{l_1}/X_1)W_2(q_{b_2}q_{l_2}/X_2)
-W_1(q_{b_1}q_{l_1}/Y_1)W_2(q_{b_2}q_{l_2}/Y_2).
\qquad\text{(73)}
\]

All four formal scales are \(\ge Z^L\) and extracted products stay equal. Retain empty rectangles until cancellation.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.4}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.4}

\hypertarget{first-poisson-transform}{%
\paragraph{5.4 First Poisson transform}\label{first-poisson-transform}}

Set \(H=Z^m\), \(X=Z^A\) and majorize rows by fixed nonnegative radial Schwartz \(\Phi_1\); central squared normalization is \(X^{-1}\). Zero frequency requires every prime's net sextic exponent to vanish, hence no prime occurs once. The combined ideal is powerful, norm \(O(X^2)\): \(O(X^{1+\epsilon})\) possibilities with divisor-many four-factor allocations, costing \(O(HZ^\epsilon)\le Z^{M+\epsilon}\).

For nonzero frequency extract full common support as \(Ca,Db\), \((a,b)=(ab,CD)=1\), \(\operatorname{rad}C=\operatorname{rad}D\). Put

\[
c=\log_Zq_C,\ d=\log_Zq_D,\ p=\log_Zq_{\operatorname{rad}C}.
\]

Let \(r\) contain common primes of nonzero net exponent modulo six, \(R=\log_Zq_r\), with primitive common character \(\xi_r\). Expand the remaining common mask by \(e\mid\operatorname{rad}C/r\), \(E=\log_Zq_e\); thus \(R+E\le p\).

For the allocated convolution coefficients \(A_C(a),A_D(b)\), residue-class Poisson gives, up to a frozen scalar of modulus at most one,

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.10}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.10}

\[
\frac{H}{Xq_e\sqrt{q_r}}
\sum_{h\ne0}\mathfrak g_{\xi_r}(r,h)
\sum_{(a,b)=1}\frac{A_C(a)\overline{A_D(b)}}{\sqrt{q_aq_b}}
\tau_C(a)\overline{\tau_D(b)}R(a,b)
\mathfrak g(a,h)\overline{\mathfrak g(b,-h)}
\widehat\Phi_1\!\left(\frac{Hq_h}{q_eq_rq_aq_b}\right).
\qquad\text{(74)}
\]

Here \(\mathfrak g_{\xi_r}\) is the normalized primitive Gauss sum, of modulus at most one, and one consistent orientation is

\[
\tau_C(a)=\tau(a)\chi_a(er)\xi_r(a),\qquad
\tau_D(b)=\tau(b)\chi_b(er)\overline{\xi_r(b)}.
\]

Indeed \(k=ek'\) and Poisson modulo \(rab\) give \(H/(q_eq_rq_aq_b)\); three Gauss sums restore \(\sqrt{q_rq_aq_b}\) and central normalization supplies \(X^{-1}\). Reciprocity gives \(R(a,b)\). Full displayed moving length becomes

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.11}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.11}

\[
\widetilde q=q+R+E.
\qquad\text{(75)}
\]

Old moving zeros puncture both products, so discard colliding allocations first; new common support is disjoint even when exponents cancel.

The natural first dual scale has logarithmic length

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.12}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.12}

\[
K_0=2A-c-d+R+E-m.
\qquad\text{(76)}
\]

Retain full smooth dyads \(q_h\asymp Z^K\), \(K\le K_0+\xi\). Separate only row and two \textbf{whole-product} norms with one Fourier measure; expand \(R(a,b)\) on its ray group. Before Cauchy insert \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.19}{(15)}'s full \(\sum_{s\mid a,b}\mu(s)\), and put \(s_0=\log_Zq_s\).

The two resulting positive Gauss norms have the form

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.13}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.13}

\[
\mathcal N_C=
\sum_h\omega_K(q_h/Z^K)
\left|Z^{-(A-c)/2}\sum_{s\mid a}A_C(a)\tau'_C(a)q_a^{it}\mathfrak g(a,h)\right|^2,
\qquad\text{(77)}
\]

and similarly for \(D\). Within each norm the profiles and masks are common; row dependence is a bounded scalar. Coefficient \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.9}{(73)} retains a common whole-product character and norm power.

A sufficient estimate is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.14}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.14}

\[
\mathcal N_C\ll Z^{A-c+\widetilde q+B_c-s_0+\epsilon},\qquad
B_c=\frac{(3c-5d-R)_+}{6}.
\qquad\text{(78)}
\]

The complete exponent balance is

\[
\underbrace{m-A-R/2-E}_{\text{Poisson prefactor}}
+\underbrace{p+s_0}_{\text{common-support and divisor counts}}
+\frac12\{A-c+\widetilde q+B_c-s_0+A-d+\widetilde q+B_d-s_0\}
\]

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.15}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.15}

\[
=M+\frac12\{B_c+B_d-(c+d-2p-R)\}\le M.
\qquad\text{(79)}
\]

Locally at multiplicities \(i\ge j\ge1\), put \(r_p=1_{6\nmid i-j}\). The contribution is \(\le(3i-5j-r_p)_+/6\), compared with nonnegative \(i+j-2-r_p\). When the former is positive, six times their difference is \(3i+11j-12-5r_p\ge0\). Subadditivity of positive parts proves \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.15}{(79)}. For fixed radical, Rankin and divisor bounds give \(Z^\epsilon\) allowed powers and allocations, verifying all label counts.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.5}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.5}

\hypertarget{second-transform-and-width-decrease}{%
\paragraph{5.5 Second transform and width decrease}\label{second-transform-and-width-decrease}}

Set \(a_0=A-c\), \(D_0=d+(K_0-K)\), and

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.16}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.16}

\[
J=D_0-c,\qquad g=J_++\sigma,
\qquad\text{(80)}
\]

Choose small width step \(\sigma>0\). Enlarge the positive Gauss norm to a smooth ball of length \(K+g\), adding its zero row. By \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.12}{(8)}, only sixth-power columns contribute: \(O(Z^{a_0/6+\epsilon})\) columns, Gauss magnitude \(\le Z^{a_0/2}\), hence centrally normalized square \(Z^{a_0/3+\epsilon}\). Nonempty \(s\mid a\) forces \(s_0\le a_0/6+O(1/\log Z)\), so \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.14}{(78)} covers it.

For the remaining norm, \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.14}{(10)} and lattice Poisson give the exact expansion

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.17}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.17}

\[
Z^{K+g-a_0}\sum_{u,v}
\frac{B(u)\overline{B(v)}\tau_1(u)\overline{\tau_1(v)}q_u^{it}q_v^{-it}}{\sqrt{q_uq_v}}
\sum_jF(u,v;j)\widehat\Phi_2\!\left(\frac{Z^{K+g}q_j}{q_uq_v}\right),
\qquad\text{(81)}
\]

Here \(B\) retains the whole rectangle difference and \(s\mid u,v\). At \(j=0\), \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.15}{(11)} forces \(u=v\), giving exponent \(K+g-s_0\) and excess over \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.14}{(78)}

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.18}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.18}

\[
(A-M)-D_0-B_c+g\le (A-M)+\sigma+O(\xi).
\qquad\text{(82)}
\]

Since \(D_0\ge-\xi\), \(-D_0+(D_0-c)_+\le O(\xi)\); thus padded \(A\le M+2\xi\) suffices.

For \(j\ne0\), retain full-kernel dyads through length \(2a_0-K-g+\xi\), then extract common support \(u=D_2a,v=E_2b\). Put

\[
c_2=\log_Zq_{D_2},\ d_2=\log_Zq_{E_2},\ b_2=(c_2+d_2)/2,
\]

\[
p_2=\log_Zq_{\operatorname{rad}D_2},\quad g_2=\log_Zq_{(D_2,E_2)}.
\]

At equal non-six-divisible multiplicities, split divided frequencies into units and nonunits, whose radical lengths are \(t_2,V\). By \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.17}{(13)}--\protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.18}{(14)},

\[
j=(D_2,E_2)V_{\rm id}h',\qquad
|F(D_2,E_2;j)|\le Z^{g_2-t_2},
\]

where \(V_{\rm id}\) is the product of the nonunit primes. Set the scalar to zero off its partition before enlarging rows. New row and displayed moving lengths are

\[
m'=2a_0-K-g-g_2-V,\quad q'=\widetilde q+t_2+V,
\]

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.19}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.19}

\[
M'=m'+q'=M+J-g-g_2+t_2\le M-\sigma.
\qquad\text{(83)}
\]

Here \(g_2\ge t_2\). Sixth-divisible fixed factors give a common puncture with retained ray phase, without a new pair-dependent mask.

Since \(s\mid u,v\) lies in the second common radical, its count is \(Z^{p_2-s_0+\epsilon}\). The ledger is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.20}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.20}

\[
\underbrace{K+g-a_0}_{\text{Poisson and roots}}
+\underbrace{p_2-s_0}_{\text{common supports}}
+\underbrace{g_2-t_2}_{\text{correlation}}
-\underbrace{b_2}_{\text{remaining central conversion}},
\qquad\text{(84)}
\]

so the inner residual products are allowed exponent

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.21}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.21}

\[
M'+\Delta_{\rm child},\qquad \Delta_{\rm child}=b_2-p_2+B_c\ge0.
\qquad\text{(85)}
\]

Separate \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.17}{(81)}'s kernel and inverse roots only in row and whole-product norms. Remove residual coprimality by \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.19}{(15)}; for each prime of its divisor \(t\), use

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.22}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.22}

\[
\mathbf1_{p\mid l_1l_2}=\mathbf1_{p\mid l_1}+\mathbf1_{p\mid l_2}
-\mathbf1_{p\mid l_1}\mathbf1_{p\mid l_2}.
\qquad\text{(86)}
\]

In each selected factor put \(l_i=pl_i'\) without \((l_i',p)=1\). The common character supplies a scalar, dividing corresponding scales in both rectangles by \(P\); mask and norm power remain common. On \(q_t\asymp Z^{t_-}\), each side extracts total length \(r_j\ge t_-+O(1/\log Z)\). Central factors \(Z^{-r_j/2}\) cancel the \(Z^{t_-}\) count; allocations cost divisors.

Both children are the original whole-product twist times \(\chi_n((D_2,E_2)V_{\rm id}h')\), differing by \(\Theta\), so either both induce a member of \(\Theta\) or neither. In the latter case apply Cauchy, then split centered children, discard empty annuli and rescale bounded subunit annuli. The completed theorem at smaller width \(M'+O(\xi)\) gives \(Z^{M'+\epsilon}\), within \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.21}{(85)}, with common polynomial masks. It remains to treat fixed-character children.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.6}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.6}

\hypertarget{exceptional-children}{%
\paragraph{5.6 Exceptional children}\label{exceptional-children}}

Outside fixed support, exceptionality prescribes \(v_p(h')\bmod6\), so \(h'=h_0v^6\) for fixed sixth-power-free \(h_0\). Let \(v_1\) be radical length of nonunit common multiplicity-one primes, \(f=2v_1\). Their fixed row valuation is two, forcing \(v_p(h')\equiv4\pmod6\). Old moving support is disjoint by its zeros, hence \(q_{h_0}\ge Z^{4v_1}\). The weaker sufficient count is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.23}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.23}

\[
\#\{h'\text{ exceptional}\}\ll Z^{(m'-f)/6+\epsilon}.
\qquad\text{(87)}
\]

This covers all \(\Theta\) and positively added rows; a forced \(h_0\) that does not fit gives an empty range.

After \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.22}{(86)}, paired normalized convolution volume is \(\ll Z^{a_0-b_2+\epsilon}\). Its excess, with \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.23}{(87)}, over \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.21}{(85)} is

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.24}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.24}

\[
\frac{m'-f}{6}+a_0-b_2-(M'+\Delta_{\rm child})
=A-\frac56M-F_1-F_2,
\qquad\text{(88)}
\]

where

\[
F_1=\frac c6+\frac{5D_0}{6}+\frac{\widetilde q}{6}+B_c-\frac{5g}{6},
\]

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.25}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.25}

\[
F_2=2b_2-\frac56g_2-p_2+t_2+\frac V6+\frac f6.
\qquad\text{(89)}
\]

There are the two local lower bounds

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.26}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.26}

\[
F_1\ge\frac23c-\frac56\sigma-O(\xi),\qquad F_2\ge\frac23b_2.
\qquad\text{(90)}
\]

If \(D_0\ge c\), substitution gives \(F_1=c+\widetilde q/6+B_c-5\sigma/6\). Otherwise \(\widetilde q\ge R\) implies \(\widetilde q+6B_c\ge(3c-5D_0)_+-O(\xi)\); use \(c+5D_0+(3c-5D_0)_+\ge4c\).

For \(F_2\), the local contributions, in units of \(\log_ZP\), are:

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{Common multiplicities; divided frequency}&F_2&b_2\\\hline
i=i,\ 6\nmid i,\ \text{unit}&7i/6&i\\
i=i,\ 6\nmid i,\ \text{nonunit}&(7i-5)/6+\tfrac13\mathbf1_{i=1}&i\\
i=i,\ 6\mid i&7i/6-1&i\\
i>j,\ 6\mid j,\ \text{unit}&i+j/6-1&(i+j)/2
\end{array}
\]

The middle column is always \(\ge2/3\) of the last, with equality at nonunit multiplicity one; these exhaust \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.17}{(13)}--\protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:A.18}{(14)}.

For \(A\le5M/6\), \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.24}{(88)}--\protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.26}{(90)} finish the uncentered stage, with terminal loss \(5\sigma/6+O(\xi)\).

For the centered stage, the common-character calculation gives, on an exceptional row, a coefficient of the form

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.27}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.27}

\[
\vartheta(l_1)\vartheta(l_2)\mathbf1_{(l_1l_2,R_*)=1}q_{l_1l_2}^{it}\mathcal D_b(l_1,l_2).
\qquad\text{(91)}
\]

The common mask contains all redundant natural zeros. Before final extraction, one paired side has all four formal scales at least

\[
Z^{L-c-\min(c_2,d_2)}.
\]

Set \(r=(L-c-\min(c_2,d_2))_+\). If that side extracts \(r_1<r\), \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.2}{(66)} saves \(Z^{-(r-r_1)}\); otherwise use volume. Divisor counts satisfy

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.28}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.28}

\[
t_--r_1-r_2-(r-r_1)_+\le-r
\qquad\text{(92)}
\]

so all allocated paired products cost \(Z^{a_0-b_2-r+\epsilon}\). Triangle before the full divisor sum loses this saving.

With \(v=c+\min(c_2,d_2)\) and \(b_2\ge\min(c_2,d_2)\), the remaining deficit is at most

\hypertarget{cf:eq:D.29}{}

\label{cf:eq:D.29}

\[
A-\frac56M-\frac23v-(L-v)_++\frac56\sigma+O(\xi)
\le A-M+\frac56\sigma+O(\xi).
\qquad\text{(93)}
\]

The maximum over \(v\ge0\) occurs at \(v=L=M/4\); padded \(A\le M+2\xi\) leaves only a small terminal loss.

\hypertarget{cf:sec:D.7}{}

\label{cf:sec:D.7}

\hypertarget{closing-the-finite-descent}{%
\paragraph{5.7 Closing the finite descent}\label{closing-the-finite-descent}}

Choose small floor \(\rho>0\), step \(\sigma>0\), then padding \(\xi>0\). At \(M\le\rho\), reflection and counting in \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.8}{(72)} lose at most \(\rho+2\xi\). Above it, \protect\hyperlink{cf:eq:D.19}{(83)} decreases width by \(\sigma\) before enclosure; \(\xi\ll\sigma\) leaves decrease \(\ge\sigma/2\). Depth is at most \(D=2+\lceil2M_{\max}/\sigma\rceil\).
